<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

For a discrete group $G$, its group von Neumann algebra is $$L(G)=\{\lambda(g):g\in G\}'',\qquad
 (\lambda(g)\xi)(h)=\xi(g^{-1}h),\quad \xi\in\ell^2(G),$$ with canonical trace $\tau(x)=\langle x\delta_e,\delta_e\rangle$. Write $\mathbb F_n$ for the free group on $n$ generators and $\mathbb F_\infty$ for the free group on countably infinitely many generators. The free group factors arose in Murray and von Neumann’s foundational work (Murray and Neumann 1943) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[murray_vonneumann1943](#ref-murray_vonneumann1943)]. Their nonhyperfiniteness distinguished them from the hyperfinite factor, but did not distinguish their ranks; see the historical account in (Rădulescu 1992, 518) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[radulescu1992](#ref-radulescu1992)]. The question whether $L(\mathbb F_m)$ and $L(\mathbb F_n)$ are isomorphic for distinct $m,n\geq2$ became a central problem in the classification of finite factors. Dykema’s introduction (Dykema 1994, 123) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[dykema1994](#ref-dykema1994)] records the early history and credits Kadison with raising the rank-isomorphism question.

Free probability supplied a way to study these factors through their generating distributions. Voiculescu developed the reduced-free-product framework (Voiculescu 1985) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[voiculescu1985](#ref-voiculescu1985)] and its semicircular and circular models (Voiculescu 1990) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[voiculescu1990](#ref-voiculescu1990)]. His compression results showed that the fundamental group of $L(\mathbb F_\infty)$ contains every positive rational, and Rădulescu extended this to every positive real number (Rădulescu 1992) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[radulescu1992](#ref-radulescu1992)]. Dykema and Rădulescu independently constructed the interpolated factors $L(\mathbb F_r)$ for real $r>1$ and established their amplification calculus (Dykema 1994; Rădulescu 1994) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[dykema1994](#ref-dykema1994), [radulescu1994](#ref-radulescu1994)]. These results relate changes of rank to normalized corners. They also give a dichotomy: the interpolated free group factors are either all isomorphic or pairwise nonisomorphic. The version including the infinite parameter is due to Rădulescu (Rădulescu 1994, Corollary 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[radulescu1994](#ref-radulescu1994)]; see also the precise restatement in (Goldbring and Pi 2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[goldbring_pi2025](#ref-goldbring_pi2025)].

The amplification calculus thus links rank to corner size, while the dichotomy reduces the classification to two alternatives. To choose the isomorphic alternative, it suffices to construct an isomorphism between two distinct finite ranks. Our constructive result is the following rank comparison.

<a id="stmt-thm-rank-step"></a>
<div id="thm:rank-step" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Isomorphism of consecutive ranks). *For every integer $n\geq3$ there is a unital trace-preserving normal $*$-isomorphism $$L(\mathbb F_n)\cong L(\mathbb F_{n+1}).$$*

</div>

The construction gives a freely generating Haar $n$-tuple inside $L(\mathbb F_{n+1})$. Applying it at ranks three and four, and then using the established amplification formula, gives the headline conclusion.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *There exists a unital normal trace-preserving $*$-isomorphism $$\Phi:L(\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow L(\mathbb F_3).$$*

</div>

This gives an affirmative solution to the free group factor isomorphism problem. Together with the classical dichotomy, this proves that all interpolated free group factors, including the infinite-rank factor, are isomorphic. Their fundamental groups are therefore $\mathbb R_{>0}$. We state and prove these consequences in Corollary <a href="#cor:all-interpolated" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:all-interpolated">17</a>; the constructive rank comparison itself does not use interpolation.

Free entropy offered another approach to distinguishing ranks. A free semicircular $n$-tuple generates $L(\mathbb F_n)$ (Voiculescu 1990, Remarks 1.10–1.11) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[voiculescu1990](#ref-voiculescu1990)] and has free entropy dimension $\delta=n$ (Voiculescu 2002b, secs. 2.4(c),(d)) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[voiculescu2002entropy](#ref-voiculescu2002entropy)]. Voiculescu asked whether $\delta$ of a finite self-adjoint tuple depends only on the tracial von Neumann algebra it generates (Voiculescu 2002b, sec. 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[voiculescu2002entropy](#ref-voiculescu2002entropy)]. Such invariance would distinguish the finite ranks. Our isomorphisms give the opposite conclusion: Corollary <a href="#cor:entropy-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:entropy-dimensions">18</a> gives a negative answer and proves the same failure for $\delta_0,\delta^*$, and $\delta^\star$. Each of the four dimensions attains every integer value $n\geq2$ on finite self-adjoint generating tuples of $L(\mathbb F_2)$. The proof transports semicircular tuples for the microstates dimensions $\delta,\delta_0$ and group-algebra tuples for the nonmicrostates dimensions $\delta^*,\delta^\star$; the two tuple families need not coincide.

Fix $n\geq3$. We begin with a freely generating Haar tuple $(A_1,\ldots,A_n,C)$ in $L(\mathbb F_{n+1})$ and set $S=\arg(C)$ using bounded Borel functional calculus. For $g\in\mathbb F_n$, let $A_g$ denote the corresponding word in $A_1,\ldots,A_n$. Conjugates $A_gSA_g^*$ drive a polynomial differential equation in $C^*(A_1,\ldots,A_n,S)$. A formal trace identity and analyticity of the actual solutions’ moments prove moment preservation. The resulting maps descend through the relations among the generators, preserve the operator norm, and extend to normal trace-preserving automorphisms.

The coefficients are finitely supported real vectors $h_j\in\ell^2(\mathbb F_n)$, extended to group words by the cocycle rule $$D_{x_j}=h_j,\qquad D_{gb}=D_g+\lambda(g)D_b,
 \qquad \mathbb F_n=\langle x_1,\ldots,x_n\rangle.$$ After trace preservation has been established, a free-sum estimate gives two bounds for the time-one automorphism $\beta_1$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow">8</a>, with $w\in\mathbb F_n$: $$\left\lVert \beta_1(A_j)-A_j\right\rVert\leq3\pi\|h_j\|_{\ell^2},\qquad
 \left\lVert \beta_1(A_w)-CA_w\right\rVert\leq3\pi\|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}.$$ Thus small generator coefficients keep the tuple nearly fixed, while a cocycle value near $\delta_e$ makes a selected word approximate $CA_w$.

The coefficient problem is solved in Lemma <a href="#lem:small-cocycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-cocycle">12</a>. For each $m\geq1$, we choose a word $w_m$ with $m$ selected nonidentity prefixes $p_1,\ldots,p_m$ forming a free family. With $h_j=0$ for $j\geq2$, its cocycle value is $D_{w_m}=T_mh_1$, where $T_m=1+\sum_{j=1}^m\lambda(p_j)$. An explicit Catalan moment count shows that the spectral measures of $T_mT_m^*/m$ converge to a measure with no atom at zero. A truncated inverse of $T_mT_m^*$ then gives a small $\ell^2$ vector whose image under $T_m$ is close to $\delta_e$. A real-symmetry argument and a final truncation give finitely supported real coefficients, as required by the polynomial flow.

A free-group basis change now yields an automorphism moving each $A_j$ arbitrarily little in operator norm while making a word in the moved $A_j$’s approximate the *old* variable $C$; this is Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">15</a>. We iterate this step, choosing each future error budget only after the current approximating word has been fixed. The first $n$ coordinates converge in norm, so their joint Haar distribution persists. The complementary coordinate may vary at every stage. Generation is proved separately: the word algebra of the limiting tuple is dense in the ambient tracial $L^2$ space, and the trace-preserving conditional expectation then identifies its von Neumann closure with the entire factor.

The analytic and algebraic ingredients have established predecessors. Voiculescu’s cyclomorphy theorem exponentiates trace-preserving polynomial vector fields on algebraically free self-adjoint generators (Voiculescu 2002a, Theorem 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[voiculescu2002cyclomorphy](#ref-voiculescu2002cyclomorphy)]. Our flow uses Haar unitaries and a fixed bounded logarithm, so the descent through their relations is proved directly from moments. Guionnet and Shlyakhtenko’s free monotone transport constructs isomorphisms by analytic changes of variables for suitable perturbations of semicircular systems (Guionnet and Shlyakhtenko 2014) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[guionnet_shlyakhtenko2014](#ref-guionnet_shlyakhtenko2014)]; the present argument uses small automorphisms and an iteration that changes the size of a generating tuple. The cocycle product rule is the coefficient form of Fox’s free differential calculus (Fox 1953, sec. 1, equation $(1.2)'$) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[fox1953](#ref-fox1953)]. The support-independent norm estimate for free sums uses the creation, annihilation, and diagonal decomposition underlying reduced-free-product Khintchine inequalities (Ricard and Xu 2006, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ricard_xu2006](#ref-ricard_xu2006)]. We give the estimates and moment arguments needed here in full.

A recent preprint of Shlyakhtenko announces identifications of specified Fuchsian group factors with interpolated free group factors (Shlyakhtenko 2026) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[shlyakhtenko2026fuchsian](#ref-shlyakhtenko2026fuchsian)]. Its main construction finds a free complement to a commutator inside $L(\mathbb F_2)$, using finite automorphisms and a limiting generation argument. In both constructions the limiting distribution and the generation of the ambient algebra require separate attention. Here the small-cocycle estimate enables absorption of an additional free generator, and the adaptive error budgets preserve the approximations needed for generation. No result from that preprint is used below.

Section <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a> supplies the free-probability and representation facts used in the construction. Sections <a href="#sec:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flow">3</a> and <a href="#sec:cocycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cocycle">4</a> construct the automorphisms and their coefficients, and Section <a href="#sec:generation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:generation">5</a> carries out the limiting argument. Section <a href="#sec:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:amplification">6</a> proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a> and its classical consequences. Section <a href="#sec:entropy-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy-dimensions">7</a> proves the generator dependence of free entropy dimensions.

<a id="sec:prelim"></a>
### Tracial preliminaries and a free-sum estimate

Throughout, $(M,\tau)$ is a von Neumann algebra with faithful normal tracial state. We write $$\left\lVert x\right\rVert_2=\tau(x^*x)^{1/2},\qquad \left\lVert x\right\rVert=\text{operator norm of }x.$$ The Hilbert space completion of $M$ in $\left\lVert \cdot\right\rVert_2$ is $L^2(M,\tau)$, with inner product $\langle x,y\rangle=\tau(y^*x)$, linear in the first variable. Left multiplication gives its faithful normal representation of $M$. A $*$-isomorphism is a bijective complex-linear map preserving multiplication and adjoints. A linear map between von Neumann algebras is *normal* if it is ultraweakly continuous. We use standard finite von Neumann algebra facts, including bounded Borel functional calculus, Kaplansky density, and the trace-preserving conditional expectation onto a von Neumann subalgebra. The expectation extends to the orthogonal projection of $L^2(M,\tau)$ onto the subalgebra’s $L^2$ space. Section <a href="#sec:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:amplification">6</a> recalls the amplification convention separately.

A family $(B_i)_{i\in I}$ of unital subalgebras is *free* if, for every integer $r\geq1$ and every choice of elements satisfying the conditions below, $$\tau(b_1\cdots b_r)=0
 \quad\text{whenever}\quad
 b_j\in B_{i_j},\quad \tau(b_j)=0,\quad
 i_j\ne i_{j+1}\ (1\leq j<r).$$ Freeness of unital $*$-algebras passes to their generated von Neumann algebras. Indeed, bounded strong approximation gives bounded $L^2$ approximation in a finite tracial algebra; telescoping a product shows continuity of its trace under such approximations. Centering the approximants proves the assertion. Freeness also persists when disjoint collections of the free components are grouped: their algebraic spans consist of scalars and reduced products of centered elements, and the same definition applies to the resulting reduced products.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Haar tuples and normal isomorphisms

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

**Definition 3**. A *freely generating Haar $d$-tuple* in $M$ is a tuple of unitaries $U=(U_1,\ldots,U_d)$ such that $W^*(U)=M$ and $$\tau(U_g)=0\qquad(g\in\mathbb F_d\setminus\{e\}).$$ Here $U_g$ is the evaluation of the reduced group word $g$ in $U$, with inverse letters evaluated as adjoints.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-haar-identification"></a>
<div id="lem:haar-identification" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Identification by a generating Haar tuple). *For a freely generating Haar $d$-tuple $U$ in $M$, the assignment $\lambda(x_j)\mapsto U_j$ extends to a unital normal trace-preserving $*$-isomorphism $L(\mathbb F_d)\to M$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-haar-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-haar-identification</summary>

*Proof.* The vectors $U_g$, indexed by $g\in\mathbb F_d$, are orthonormal because $\tau(U_h^*U_g)=\tau(U_{h^{-1}g})=\mathbf 1_{g=h}$. They span $L^2(M,\tau)$. To see this, let $\eta$ be orthogonal to their span. Choose $\eta_m\in M$ converging to $\eta$ in $L^2$. The normal functionals $f_m(x)=\tau(\eta_m^*x)$ converge in norm to $f(x)=\langle x,\eta\rangle$, since $\left\lVert f_m-f\right\rVert_{M^*}\leq\left\lVert \eta_m-\eta\right\rVert_2$. The space $M_*$ of normal linear functionals is norm closed in $M^*$, so $f$ is normal. It vanishes on the ultraweakly dense word algebra and therefore on all of $M$. Thus $\eta=0$.

Consequently the map $V:\ell^2(\mathbb F_d)\to L^2(M,\tau)$ with $V\delta_g=U_g$ is unitary. It intertwines $\lambda(x_j)$ with left multiplication by $U_j$. Conjugation by $V$ identifies their double commutants. The double commutant on the right is the faithful normal left representation of $W^*(U)=M$, so this spatial identification gives the claimed isomorphism. It preserves the trace because $V\delta_e=1$. ◻

</details>

In particular, two freely generating Haar tuples of the same size in $M$ are related by a normal trace-preserving automorphism. The component algebras of a Haar tuple are free: expanding centered Laurent polynomials reduces the assertion to the nonidentity group-word trace, and bounded approximation gives freeness of the von Neumann closures.

For completeness, $L(\mathbb F_d)$ is a $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor for $d\geq2$. Its canonical trace is $\tau(x)=\langle x\delta_e,\delta_e\rangle$, which is faithful and normal. Every nonidentity reduced word has infinitely many distinct conjugates: choose a letter $a$ whose powers cause no cancellation at either end of $a^kga^{-k}$. At most two of the $2d$ letters are excluded, and the reduced lengths then tend to infinity. Fourier coefficients of a central element are constant on conjugacy classes and form an $\ell^2$ vector, so every coefficient off the identity vanishes. Faithfulness of the tracial representation implies that the center consists of scalars. The infinitely many orthonormal group unitaries show that the factor is infinite-dimensional.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Conjugates of a bounded logarithm

Fix a freely generating Haar tuple $(A_1,\ldots,A_n,C)$, let $\Gamma=\mathbb F_n$, and write $A_g$ for the word in the first $n$ variables indexed by $g\in\Gamma$. Put <a id="eq:logarithm"></a>$$\label{eq:logarithm}
 S=\arg(C),\qquad S_g=A_gSA_g^*\quad(g\in\Gamma),$$ where the argument takes values in $(-\pi,\pi]$. The bounded Borel functional calculus for normal operators defines this logarithm in $W^*(C)$; see (Haase 2020, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[haase2020](#ref-haase2020)]. Since the spectral distribution of $C$ is uniform on the circle, $$S=S^*,\quad \left\lVert S\right\rVert\leq\pi,\quad \tau(S)=0,\quad e^{\mathrm iS}=C,
 \qquad W^*(S)=W^*(C).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-conjugates"></a>
<div id="lem:conjugates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Freeness of the conjugate components). *The algebras $A_gW^*(C)A_g^*$, $g\in\Gamma$, form a free family. In particular, each $S_g$ is free from the von Neumann algebra generated by all $S_b$ with $b\ne g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conjugates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conjugates</summary>

*Proof.* Consider a product of centered elements $A_{g_j}c_jA_{g_j}^*$, with $c_j\in W^*(C)$ and $g_j\ne g_{j+1}$. Cyclicity of the trace rewrites its trace as $$\tau\bigl(c_1 A_{g_1^{-1}g_2}c_2\cdots
          A_{g_{r-1}^{-1}g_r}c_r A_{g_r^{-1}g_1}\bigr).$$ All interior group elements are nonidentity and hence centered. If the last group element is the identity, omit it; otherwise it too is centered. Freeness of $W^*(A_1,\ldots,A_n)$ and $W^*(C)$ makes the displayed trace zero. For a single factor this is simply $\tau(c_1)=0$. The assertion about the other components follows by grouping free subalgebras. ◻

</details>

For a monomial in $A_j,A_j^*$ and $S$, define its *label* as the product in $\Gamma$ of its group symbols, read in order with each occurrence of $S$ ignored.

<a id="stmt-lem-labels"></a>
<div id="lem:labels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Group labels and traces). *A monomial with nonidentity label has trace zero. A monomial with identity label can be expressed as a product of variables $S_g$ (or as the constant $1$).*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels</summary>

*Proof.* For the first assertion, combine consecutive group symbols and consecutive powers of $S$. Remove identity group factors. Split each polynomial in $S$ into its scalar trace and a centered part. A term with all alternating factors centered has trace zero by freeness of the group algebra and $W^*(S)$. Removing a scalar factor permits a reduction to fewer alternating factors, without changing the total group label. Induction on the number of factors ends with either a centered alternating product or a single group element whose label remains nonidentity. Both have trace zero.

For the second assertion, successively use $A_g S=S_g A_g$ to move every group factor to the right. The final group factor is exactly the label, and is therefore $1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### A bound independent of support size

For a finitely supported real function $k$ on $\Gamma$, set $$s(k)=\sum_{g\in\Gamma}k(g)S_g.$$ The coefficient norm $\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{\ell^2}$ below is distinguished from the tracial $L^2$ norm $\left\lVert \cdot\right\rVert_2$ of operators. The next estimate uses the creation, annihilation, and diagonal decomposition underlying free-product Khintchine inequalities; see Ricard and Xu (Ricard and Xu 2006, sec. 2, Lemmas 2.1–2.3 and Corollary 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[ricard_xu2006](#ref-ricard_xu2006)]. We include the scalar self-adjoint argument to give an explicit constant independent of the number of summands.

<a id="stmt-lem-free-sum"></a>
<div id="lem:free-sum" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:free-sum"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Norm of a free sum). *For every such $k$, $$\label{eq:free-sum}
 \left\lVert s(k)\right\rVert\leq K\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{\ell^2},\qquad K=3\pi.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-sum</summary>

<a id="eq:general-free-sum"></a>
*Proof.* We prove the more general bound $$\label{eq:general-free-sum}
 \left\lVert \sum_j z_j\right\rVert
 \leq 2\Bigl(\sum_j\left\lVert z_j\right\rVert_2^2\Bigr)^{1/2}
       +\max_j\left\lVert z_j\right\rVert$$ for a finite free family of subalgebras and centered self-adjoint $z_j$ in the respective components.

Work in the tracial $L^2$ space of their generated algebra. Freeness identifies this space with the orthogonal sum of the scalar vector and the reduced centered tensor spaces, whose successive component indices are different. To check the identification, take inner products of reduced centered words. If the two letters at the joining point belong to different components, freeness gives zero. If they belong to the same component, split their product into its trace and its centered part; the latter again has zero contribution, while the scalar term shortens both words. Iteration gives orthogonality for different index sequences and the tensor-product inner product for matching sequences. The reduced word span is dense by the argument used in Lemma <a href="#lem:haar-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:haar-identification">4</a>.

Let $P_j$ project onto the reduced tensors beginning with component $j$, and let $L_{z_j}$ denote left multiplication. On $(1-P_j)L^2$, multiplication prefixes $z_j$, so $$B_j=P_jL_{z_j}(1-P_j),\qquad \left\lVert B_j\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert z_j\right\rVert_2.$$ The ranges of the $B_j$ are orthogonal. For every vector $\xi$ this gives $$\left\lVert \sum_jB_j\xi\right\rVert^2
   =\sum_j\left\lVert B_j\xi\right\rVert^2
   \leq\Bigl(\sum_j\left\lVert z_j\right\rVert_2^2\Bigr)\left\lVert \xi\right\rVert^2.$$ The blocks in the opposite direction are $B_j^*$, because $z_j$ is self-adjoint. The block $(1-P_j)L_{z_j}(1-P_j)$ is zero. The remaining diagonal blocks $P_jL_{z_j}P_j$ act on mutually orthogonal subspaces; their sum has norm at most $\max_j\left\lVert z_j\right\rVert$. Summing these three parts proves <a href="#eq:general-free-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-free-sum">[eq:general-free-sum]</a>, since the left representation is faithful.

Apply this bound to $z_g=k(g)S_g$. Both $\left\lVert S_g\right\rVert_2$ and $\left\lVert S_g\right\rVert$ are at most $\pi$, and $\max_g\left\lvert k(g)\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{\ell^2}$. This proves <a href="#eq:free-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-sum">[eq:free-sum]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:flow"></a>
### Trace-preserving polynomial flows

Voiculescu’s exponentiation theorem turns trace-preserving polynomial vector fields on algebraically free self-adjoint generators into one-parameter automorphism groups (Voiculescu 2002a, Theorem 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[voiculescu2002cyclomorphy](#ref-voiculescu2002cyclomorphy)]. We prove the version needed here directly for Haar unitaries and a fixed bounded logarithm. The proof treats the unitary relations explicitly and derives preservation of all evaluated relations from moment invariance.

Retain the notation of Section <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a>. Thus $(A_1,\ldots,A_n,C)$ is a freely generating Haar tuple in the finite tracial von Neumann algebra $(M,\tau)$, $\Gamma=\mathbb F_n=\langle x_1,\ldots,x_n\rangle$, and $$S=\arg(C),\qquad S_g=A_gSA_g^*,\qquad
 s(k)=\sum_{g\in\Gamma}k(g)S_g.$$ Write $\mathbb R^{(\Gamma)}$ for the finitely supported real functions on $\Gamma$, and let $\delta_e\in\ell^2(\Gamma)$ be the unit vector at the identity. All coefficient vectors used in this section are finitely supported. The left regular action on them is $(\lambda(g)k)(b)=k(g^{-1}b)$.

Choose real coefficient vectors $h_1,\ldots,h_n$. There is a unique cocycle $D:\Gamma\to\mathbb R^{(\Gamma)}$ satisfying <a id="eq:flow-cocycle"></a>$$D_{x_j}=h_j,\qquad D_{gb}=D_g+\lambda(g)D_b.
 \label{eq:flow-cocycle}$$ Indeed, set $D_{x_j^{-1}}=-\lambda(x_j^{-1})h_j$ and define the value on a word by summing the shifted values of its letters. The two terms associated with an adjacent inverse pair cancel, so the definition respects reduction. The cocycle identity and uniqueness follow.

The next proposition turns small $\ell^2$ norms of the $h_j$ into small operator-norm displacements of the generators. For a word $w\in\Gamma$, a small $\|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}$ also makes the time-one image of $A_w$ close to $CA_w$.

<a id="stmt-prop-flow"></a>
<div id="prop:flow" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:flow-ode"></a>
<a id="eq:generator-displacement"></a>
<a id="eq:word-displacement"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Polynomial flow and displacement bounds). *There is a one-parameter group $(\beta_t)_{t\in\mathbb R}$ of unital trace-preserving normal $*$-automorphisms of $M$ that fixes $S$ and whose restriction to $$B=C^*(A_1,\ldots,A_n,S)$$ is point-norm continuous. Writing $A_j(t)=\beta_t(A_j)$ and evaluating group words at this tuple, the paths satisfy $$\frac{d}{dt}A_j(t)=\mathrm is_t(h_j)A_j(t),\qquad
 s_t(k)=\sum_{g\in\Gamma}k(g)A_g(t)SA_g(t)^*,\qquad A_j(0)=A_j.
 \label{eq:flow-ode}$$ The derivatives here are in operator norm. With $K=3\pi$ as in <a href="#eq:free-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-sum">[eq:free-sum]</a>, for every $t\in\mathbb R$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\lVert \beta_t(A_j)-A_j\right\rVert
 &\leq |t|K\|h_j\|_{\ell^2},
 \label{eq:generator-displacement}\\
 \left\lVert \beta_t(A_w)-\exp(\mathrm itS)A_w\right\rVert
 &\leq |t|K\|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}
 \qquad(w\in\Gamma).
 \label{eq:word-displacement}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, at $t=1$ the target in <a href="#eq:word-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-displacement">[eq:word-displacement]</a> is $CA_w$.*

</div>

We first establish a trace identity on a formal polynomial algebra. This will prove invariance along the actual flow without assuming freeness of the evolving tuple.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The formal derivation and its trace identity

Let $\mathcal P$ be the complex unital $*$-algebra generated by unitaries $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and a self-adjoint symbol $z$, subject only to these unitary and self-adjoint relations. Put $$z_g=a_gza_g^*,\qquad \sigma(k)=\sum_gk(g)z_g.$$ Here $a_g$ is the group word in the formal unitaries. Evaluation at $(A_1,\ldots,A_n,S)$ defines a $*$-homomorphism $\operatorname{ev}_0:\mathcal P\to B$; we make no assumption about its kernel.

<a id="eq:formal-derivation"></a>
<a id="eq:formal-word-derivative"></a>
Define a complex-linear derivation by the rules $$\delta z=0,\qquad
 \delta a_j=\mathrm i\sigma(h_j)a_j,\qquad
 \delta a_j^*=-\mathrm ia_j^*\sigma(h_j).
 \label{eq:formal-derivation}$$ These rules define a $*$-derivation on $\mathcal P$. The reality of $h_j$ makes $\sigma(h_j)$ self-adjoint, so the rules respect adjoints. They also respect the defining unitary relations: for example, $$\delta(a_j^*a_j)
 =-\mathrm ia_j^*\sigma(h_j)a_j+\mathrm ia_j^*\sigma(h_j)a_j=0,$$ and the calculation for $a_ja_j^*$ is the same. The identity $a_g\sigma(k)a_g^*=\sigma(\lambda(g)k)$ and the cocycle rule give $$\delta a_g=\mathrm i\sigma(D_g)a_g,
 \qquad
 \delta z_g=\mathrm i[\sigma(D_g),z_g].
 \label{eq:formal-word-derivative}$$ For instance, multiplying the formulas for $g$ and $b$ gives the formula for $gb$ with coefficient $D_g+\lambda(g)D_b$; inverse letters are covered by the unitary relations.

<a id="stmt-lem-flow-trace-identity"></a>
<div id="lem:flow-trace-identity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:universal-trace-identity"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Universal initial trace identity). *For every $p\in\mathcal P$ and every integer $r\geq1$, $$\tau\bigl(\operatorname{ev}_0(\delta^r p)\bigr)=0.
 \label{eq:universal-trace-identity}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flow-trace-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flow-trace-identity</summary>

*Proof.* First take $r=1$. Give a monomial its group label by ignoring each occurrence of $z$ and multiplying the remaining group symbols in order. Each term of $\sigma(k)$ has label $e$. The replacement rules <a href="#eq:formal-derivation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-derivation">[eq:formal-derivation]</a> consequently preserve a monomial’s label term by term. If that label is nonidentity, Lemma <a href="#lem:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels">6</a> makes every evaluated derivative term have trace zero.

An identity-label monomial is a polynomial in the $z_g$. To see this algebraically, repeatedly use $a_gz=z_ga_g$ to move group factors to the right; the final group factor is the identity. Because these are identities in $\mathcal P$ and $\delta$ is a derivation on that algebra, differentiating this rewriting gives the same result as differentiating the original monomial. It is therefore enough to consider $p=F(z_g:g\in I)$, with $I\subset\Gamma$ finite and $F$ a noncommutative polynomial.

For a fixed $g\in I$, remove the commuting own-variable term from the commutator in <a href="#eq:formal-word-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-word-derivative">[eq:formal-word-derivative]</a>. Its evaluated direction is $$\operatorname{ev}_0(\delta z_g)=\mathrm i[Y_g,S_g],\qquad
 Y_g=\sum_{b\ne g}D_g(b)S_b.$$ The element $Y_g$ is a bounded self-adjoint in $B_g=W^*(S_b:b\ne g)$, and $W^*(S_g)$ is free from $B_g$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:conjugates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conjugates">5</a>.

We use the following direct consequence of freeness. If a unital subalgebra $\mathcal A$ is free from a unital subalgebra $\mathcal B$ and $U\in\mathcal B$ is unitary, then $U\mathcal A U^*$ is free from $\mathcal B$. Indeed, conjugating an alternating product of centered elements from these two algebras by $U^*$ gives an alternating product of centered elements from $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$: a fixed centered $b\in\mathcal B$ becomes $U^*bU$, which is still centered in $\mathcal B$. Its trace is zero. Conjugation preserves the marginal law of each element of $\mathcal A$. Since freeness determines mixed moments from the marginal laws, it follows that replacing $S_g$ by $US_gU^*$ preserves its joint law with any fixed collection of elements of $B_g$.

Apply this observation with $U=\exp(\mathrm iuY_g)$ for real $u$. The scalar function obtained by replacing every occurrence of $S_g$ in $F$ by $\exp(\mathrm iuY_g)S_g\exp(-\mathrm iuY_g)$ and leaving all other variables fixed has constant trace. Its derivative at zero is the trace of the sum over those occurrences with the direction $\mathrm i[Y_g,S_g]$ inserted. Sum these zero derivatives over $g\in I$. The product rule and <a href="#eq:formal-word-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-word-derivative">[eq:formal-word-derivative]</a> identify the sum exactly with $\tau(\operatorname{ev}_0(\delta p))$, proving the case $r=1$. Linearity handles arbitrary finite sums of monomials.

The result just proved is the identity $\tau\circ\operatorname{ev}_0\circ\delta=0$ on all of $\mathcal P$. Since $\delta$ maps finite polynomials to finite polynomials, applying it to $\delta^{r-1}p$ proves <a href="#eq:universal-trace-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:universal-trace-identity">[eq:universal-trace-identity]</a>. In this application every derivative of every coefficient polynomial is included. The auxiliary conjugations above are used only to compute a first variation at the initial tuple; they need neither lift to motions of the $a_j$ nor be jointly integrated. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Global solutions and actual moment invariance

We now solve the polynomial equation globally in $B$ and use analyticity to turn the initial trace identity into moment invariance. For a tuple $X=(X_1,\ldots,X_n)$, let $X_g$ denote the reduced group word evaluated in a $*$-algebra using adjoints for inverse letters. This notation defines a polynomial even for nonunitary tuples, so the right side of <a href="#eq:flow-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-ode">[eq:flow-ode]</a> defines the finite polynomial vector field $$\mathcal V_j(X)=\mathrm iH_j(X)X_j,
 \qquad
 H_j(X)=\sum_g h_j(g)X_gSX_g^*.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-flow-global-ode"></a>
<div id="lem:flow-global-ode" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Global solvability in a generated subalgebra). *Let $E$ be any closed unital $C^*$-subalgebra of $M$ containing $S$, and let $V_1,\ldots,V_n$ be unitaries in $E$. The equation $X'=\mathcal V(X)$ with $X(0)=V$ has a unique global solution in $E^n$ that is smooth in norm, and all its coordinates remain unitary.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flow-global-ode"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flow-global-ode</summary>

*Proof.* Regard $E^n$, with its maximum norm, as a real Banach space. Adjunction is bounded and real-linear, so $\mathcal V$ is a finite smooth polynomial map. Its norm and its Lipschitz constant are bounded on every fixed bounded ball. Picard iteration on a sufficiently short interval gives a unique local solution; repeated differentiation makes it smooth.

Each $H_j(X)$ is self-adjoint, even before unitarity is known. Along a local solution, $$\frac{d}{dt}(X_j^*X_j)
 =-\mathrm iX_j^*H_j(X)X_j+\mathrm iX_j^*H_j(X)X_j=0.$$ Thus $X_j^*X_j=1$. Such an isometry is unitary in $M$: the projection $1-X_jX_j^*$ has trace $1-\tau(X_j^*X_j)=0$, and faithfulness makes it zero. This equality also holds in $E$, so $\left\lVert X_j\right\rVert=1$ throughout the local solution.

On this unitary trajectory, $$\left\lVert X_j'\right\rVert\leq \left\lVert S\right\rVert\sum_g|h_j(g)|.$$ If a maximal interval had a finite endpoint, this bounded derivative would make the tuple norm-Cauchy as time approached that endpoint. Its limit lies in the closed algebra $E$ and is again a unitary tuple. Local existence at the limit continues the solution, a contradiction. The same argument applies at either endpoint, giving a global solution. Uniqueness follows from local uniqueness on overlapping intervals. This continuation uses completeness and bounded derivative, not compactness of a bounded ball. ◻

</details>

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:flow-global-ode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flow-global-ode">10</a> in $E=B$ with initial tuple $A$. Let $A(t)$ be its solution and let $\operatorname{ev}_t:\mathcal P\to B$ evaluate the formal generators at $A(t),S$. Unitarity makes this a well-defined evaluation on the formal algebra for each $t$.

<a id="stmt-lem-flow-moments"></a>
<div id="lem:flow-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:flow-moments"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Moment invariance). *For every $p\in\mathcal P$ and $t\in\mathbb R$, $$\tau\bigl(\operatorname{ev}_t(p)\bigr)
 =\tau\bigl(\operatorname{ev}_0(p)\bigr).
 \label{eq:flow-moments}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flow-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flow-moments</summary>

<a id="eq:flow-iterated-derivatives"></a>
*Proof.* The differential equation and the product rule give, for every integer $r\geq0$, $$\frac{d^r}{dt^r}\operatorname{ev}_t(p)
 =\operatorname{ev}_t(\delta^r p).
 \label{eq:flow-iterated-derivatives}$$ We verify that these derivatives give an actual convergent Taylor expansion for the trace.

<a id="eq:flow-analytic-bound"></a>
Choose fixed polynomial representatives for the derivatives of the formal letters in <a href="#eq:formal-derivation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-derivation">[eq:formal-derivation]</a>. There are constants $J\geq1$ and $B_1\geq1$ such that replacing any one letter by its derivative increases monomial length by at most $J$ and produces total absolute coefficient sum at most $B_1$. These constants are finite because the $h_j$ have finite support. If a representative of $p$ has degree at most $l$, where $l\geq1$, and total absolute coefficient sum $b_0$, repeated replacement gives a representative of $\delta^r p$ with degree at most $l+rJ$ and coefficient sum at most $$b_0 B_1^r\prod_{v=0}^{r-1}(l+vJ).$$ Let $R=\max(1,\left\lVert S\right\rVert)$. All the evaluated group letters are unitary, so <a href="#eq:flow-iterated-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-iterated-derivatives">[eq:flow-iterated-derivatives]</a> implies, uniformly for real $t$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{1}{r!}
 \left\|\frac{d^r}{dt^r}\operatorname{ev}_t(p)\right\|
 &\leq b_0R^l(B_1R^J)^r
       \frac{\prod_{v=0}^{r-1}(l+vJ)}{r!} \notag\\
 &\leq b_0R^l\bigl[B_1R^J(l+J)\bigr]^r.
 \label{eq:flow-analytic-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses $l+vJ\leq(l+J)(v+1)$.

Set $f_p(t)=\tau(\operatorname{ev}_t(p))$ and $c_p=B_1R^J(l+J)$. The integral form of Taylor’s remainder and <a href="#eq:flow-analytic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-analytic-bound">[eq:flow-analytic-bound]</a> bound the remainder after degree $r$ at increment $u$ by $b_0R^l(c_p|u|)^{r+1}$. Consequently $f_p$ is real-analytic near every real time, with positive radius at least $c_p^{-1}$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:flow-trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flow-trace-identity">9</a>, all its positive-order derivatives at zero vanish. It is constant near zero and hence, by real-analytic continuation on the connected line $\mathbb R$, constant everywhere.

The constants here may depend on the fixed field and on $p$. No radius common to all polynomials or to different choices of the $h_j$ is required. The bounds used only unitarity of the solution and boundedness of $S$; freeness at positive times has not been assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Surjectivity, normal extension, and estimates

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flow-moments-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flow-moments</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow">8</a>.* For a fixed real $t$, polynomial evaluation defines a map $$\varphi_t:\operatorname{ev}_0(\mathcal P)
      \longrightarrow B,
 \qquad
 \varphi_t\bigl(\operatorname{ev}_0(p)\bigr)
      =\operatorname{ev}_t(p).$$ This is well defined. If $\operatorname{ev}_0(p)=0$, then <a href="#eq:flow-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-moments">[eq:flow-moments]</a> applied to $p^*p$ gives $$\tau\bigl(\operatorname{ev}_t(p)^*
                 \operatorname{ev}_t(p)\bigr)=0,$$ so faithfulness implies $\operatorname{ev}_t(p)=0$. Thus any additional relations at the evaluated initial tuple are respected as a consequence of actual moment invariance. The map is unital, complex-linear, multiplicative, and adjoint-preserving because formal evaluation has those properties.

For an element $b$ of a finite von Neumann algebra with faithful tracial state, $$\left\lVert b\right\rVert
 =\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau((b^*b)^k)^{1/(2k)}.$$ For completeness, the upper bound is immediate; for the lower bound, every nonzero spectral projection of $b^*b$ sufficiently near its norm has positive trace by faithfulness. Applying <a href="#eq:flow-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-moments">[eq:flow-moments]</a> to $(p^*p)^k$ therefore shows that $\varphi_t$ is isometric. It extends uniquely to a trace-preserving isometric $*$-homomorphism on $B$, with range $$B_t=C^*(A_1(t),\ldots,A_n(t),S).$$ Indeed, its range is closed and is the norm closure of the polynomials in these time-$t$ generators.

We next prove $B_t=B$, which establishes surjectivity. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:flow-global-ode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flow-global-ode">10</a> inside $B_t$ to the initial tuple $A(t)$, using the same fixed element $S$ and the same polynomial field. Its global solution, viewed in $M$, must be $u\mapsto A(t+u)$ by uniqueness. At $u=-t$ it recovers the original tuple. Thus every $A_j$ belongs to $B_t$, and $S$ already belongs to $B_t$, giving $B\subseteq B_t$. The forward construction gives the reverse inclusion. Hence $\varphi_t$ is a $C^*$-automorphism of $B$ fixing $S$.

Since $\exp(\mathrm iS)=C$, the von Neumann algebra generated by $B$ is $M$. Its vectors are therefore dense in $L^2(M,\tau)$, as in the density argument in Lemma <a href="#lem:haar-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:haar-identification">4</a>. Trace preservation and surjectivity define a Hilbert-space unitary $$U_t[b]=[\varphi_t(b)]\qquad(b\in B)$$ on $L^2(M,\tau)$, fixing $[1]$. If $L_b$ denotes left multiplication, then on the dense set of vectors coming from $B$, $$U_t L_b U_t^*=L_{\varphi_t(b)}\qquad(b\in B).$$ The faithful normal left representation identifies the von Neumann closure of $L(B)$ with $M$. Conjugation by $U_t$ therefore gives a normal $*$-automorphism $\beta_t$ of $M$ extending $\varphi_t$. It is trace-preserving because $U_t$ fixes $[1]$, and it fixes $S$. This normal extension is unique by ultraweak density of $B$.

The maps form a one-parameter group. For fixed $s$, the path $t\mapsto\varphi_s(A(t))$ satisfies the same polynomial equation, because $\varphi_s$ is a bounded $*$-homomorphism fixing $S$. Its initial tuple is $A(s)$, so uniqueness gives $$\varphi_s(A_j(t))=A_j(s+t).$$ The equality on the generators and on $S$ yields $\varphi_s\varphi_t=\varphi_{s+t}$ on $B$, and uniqueness of normal extension gives $\beta_s\beta_t=\beta_{s+t}$ on $M$. Also $\beta_0$ is the identity. Norm continuity on polynomials in the generators follows from the solution paths; density and the common isometry bound extend it to point-norm continuity on $B$. No point-norm continuity on arbitrary elements of $M$ is needed.

It remains to prove the bounds. For each finitely supported real $k$, $$s_t(k)=\beta_t(s(k)),\qquad
 \left\lVert s_t(k)\right\rVert=\left\lVert s(k)\right\rVert\leq K\|k\|_{\ell^2}$$ by <a href="#eq:free-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-sum">[eq:free-sum]</a>. Integrating $\left\lVert A_j'(t)\right\rVert\leq K\|h_j\|_{\ell^2}$ between zero and $t$ proves <a href="#eq:generator-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:generator-displacement">[eq:generator-displacement]</a>.

For a fixed $w\in\Gamma$, evaluation of <a href="#eq:formal-word-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:formal-word-derivative">[eq:formal-word-derivative]</a> along the flow gives $$\frac{d}{dt}A_w(t)=\mathrm is_t(D_w)A_w(t).$$ Moreover $s_t(\delta_e)=S$. Direct differentiation, with the order of factors retained, yields $$\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(\exp(-\mathrm itS)A_w(t)\bigr)
 =\mathrm i\exp(-\mathrm itS)\bigl(s_t(D_w)-S\bigr)A_w(t).$$ The exterior factors are unitary, so its norm is at most $K\|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}$. Integrating between zero and $t$ and multiplying on the left by $\exp(\mathrm itS)$ proves <a href="#eq:word-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-displacement">[eq:word-displacement]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cocycle"></a>
### A small cocycle with a prescribed word value

Let $n\geq 3$ and $\Gamma=\mathbb F_n$, with free generators $x_1,\ldots,x_n$. We use the left regular action on coefficient vectors, $$(\lambda(g)h)(b)=h(g^{-1}b),
  \qquad g,b\in\Gamma,$$ and write $\tau_\Gamma(X)=\langle X\delta_e,\delta_e\rangle$ for the canonical trace on $L(\Gamma)$. Given finitely supported real vectors $h_1,\ldots,h_n$, let $D$ be the cocycle determined by $$D_{x_j}=h_j,
  \qquad D_{gb}=D_g+\lambda(g)D_b.$$ The construction in this section makes both $h_1$ and $D_w-\delta_e$ small in $\ell^2(\Gamma)$, with all the other generator coefficients zero. At time one, Proposition <a href="#prop:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow">8</a> converts these two coefficient bounds into operator-norm bounds for the generator displacement and the error between the moved word and $CA_w$. The word $w$ and the support of $h_1$ are allowed to depend on the required accuracy. This coefficient rule is the cocycle form of Fox’s free differential calculus (Fox 1953, sec. 1, equations $(1.2)'$, (1.5)–(1.6), and Section 2, equations (2.1)–(2.2)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[fox1953](#ref-fox1953)]: successive letters contribute their generator coefficients translated by the preceding prefix. We use that rule explicitly below.

<a id="stmt-lem-small-cocycle"></a>
<div id="lem:small-cocycle" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:small-cocycle"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Small coefficients with a prescribed word value). *Let $n\geq 3$ and $\eta>0$. There exist a word $w\in\Gamma$ and a finitely supported real coefficient vector $h$ such that the cocycle determined by $$h_1=h,\qquad h_j=0\quad(2\leq j\leq n)$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:small-cocycle}
  \|h\|_{\ell^2}<\eta,
  \qquad
  \|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}<\eta.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Free prefix words

For $j\geq 1$, set <a id="eq:cocycle-prefixes"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-prefixes}
  a=x_1,
  \qquad b_j=x_2^j x_3 x_2^{-j},
  \qquad p_0=e,
  \qquad p_j=(a b_1)\cdots(a b_j).$$ For $m\geq 1$ also set <a id="eq:cocycle-word-operator"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-word-operator}
  w_m=p_m a,
  \qquad T_m=1+\sum_{j=1}^m\lambda(p_j).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-cocycle-prefixes"></a>
<div id="lem:cocycle-prefixes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cocycle-word-value"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Free prefixes and the cocycle value). *For each $m\geq 1$, the elements $p_1,\ldots,p_m$ freely generate a free subgroup of $\Gamma$. If $h_1=h$ and $h_j=0$ for $j\geq 2$, then $$\label{eq:cocycle-word-value}
  D_{w_m}=T_m h.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cocycle-prefixes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cocycle-prefixes</summary>

*Proof.* First consider a nonempty reduced word in $b_1,\ldots,b_m$. Combining consecutive powers with the same index writes it as $$b_{j_1}^{q_1}\cdots b_{j_l}^{q_l},
  \qquad q_i\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\},
  \qquad j_i\ne j_{i+1}.$$ Its expansion in $x_2,x_3$ is $$x_2^{j_1}x_3^{q_1}
  x_2^{j_2-j_1}x_3^{q_2}\cdots
  x_2^{j_l-j_{l-1}}x_3^{q_l}x_2^{-j_l}.$$ Every displayed $x_3$ power is nonzero, and each intervening $x_2$ power is nonzero. This word cannot reduce to the identity. Thus the $b_j$ are a free basis of the subgroup they generate. The free product decomposition $$\Gamma=\langle a\rangle *\langle x_2,\ldots,x_n\rangle$$ then shows that $a,b_1,\ldots,b_m$ freely generate their subgroup: an alternating product of nonidentity elements from $\langle a\rangle$ and $\langle b_1,\ldots,b_m\rangle$ is also reduced in the displayed free product.

In the abstract free group on $a,b_1,\ldots,b_m$, the substitution fixing $a$ and sending each $b_j$ to $a b_j$ has inverse given by $b_j\mapsto a^{-1}b_j$, again fixing $a$. Consequently $$u_j=a b_j,\qquad 1\leq j\leq m,$$ form a free family. On the abstract free group with free basis $u_1,\ldots,u_m$, define two endomorphisms by $$\varphi(u_j)=u_1\cdots u_j,
  \qquad
  \psi(u_1)=u_1,
  \qquad
  \psi(u_j)=u_{j-1}^{-1}u_j\quad (j\geq 2).$$ For every $j$, cancellation gives $$\begin{aligned}
  \psi\varphi(u_j)
    &=u_1(u_1^{-1}u_2)\cdots(u_{j-1}^{-1}u_j)=u_j,\\
  \varphi\psi(u_j)
    &=(u_1\cdots u_{j-1})^{-1}(u_1\cdots u_j)=u_j
       \quad (j\geq 2),
\end{aligned}$$ and the second identity also holds for $j=1$. Thus $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are inverse automorphisms. Since $p_j=u_1\cdots u_j$, this proves the assertion about the $p_j$.

For the cocycle assertion, the inverse rule $D_{x_j^{-1}}=-\lambda(x_j^{-1})D_{x_j}$ shows that $D$ vanishes on the subgroup generated by $x_2,\ldots,x_n$. In particular, $D_{b_j}=0$. The positive occurrences of $a$ in $w_m$ have respective prefixes $p_0,p_1,\ldots,p_m$, and there are no other occurrences of $a$ or $a^{-1}$. Applying the cocycle identity successively therefore gives $$D_{w_m}=\sum_{j=0}^m\lambda(p_j)h=T_mh.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The spectral measures of the prefix sums

Put <a id="eq:cocycle-positive-operator"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-positive-operator}
  Q_m=T_mT_m^*,
  \qquad
  \mu_m(B)=\tau_\Gamma\bigl(1_B(Q_m/m)\bigr)$$ for Borel sets $B\subseteq[0,\infty)$. These are probability measures by the spectral theorem and the normalization of $\tau_\Gamma$.

We seek a small vector $h$ with $T_mh$ close to $\delta_e$. The spectral limit below has no atom at zero. It will let us choose a small fixed cutoff in the normalized spectrum and then make $m$ large enough that the discarded projection has small trace. On the remaining spectrum, the inverse of $Q_m$ has norm at most a fixed constant times $m^{-1}$. The inverse bound controls the coefficient norm, while the discarded trace controls the approximation error. The Catalan count below is the moment calculation associated with circular systems in Voiculescu’s free-probability framework; see (Voiculescu 1985) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[voiculescu1985](#ref-voiculescu1985)] and (Voiculescu 1990, Definition 1.9 and Remark 1.11) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[voiculescu1990](#ref-voiculescu1990)]. We give the word count and the normalization of the limiting density explicitly.

<a id="stmt-lem-cocycle-spectrum"></a>
<div id="lem:cocycle-spectrum" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cocycle-limit-density"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Spectral limit of the prefix sums). *The measures $\mu_m$ converge weakly to the probability measure $$\label{eq:cocycle-limit-density}
  d\nu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}
           \,1_{(0,4)}(x)\,dx.$$ In particular, $\nu(\{0\})=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cocycle-spectrum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cocycle-spectrum</summary>

*Proof.* Write $V_j=\lambda(p_j)$ and $V_0=1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cocycle-prefixes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cocycle-prefixes">13</a> implies that a word in $V_1,\ldots,V_m$ and their inverses has trace one if its word in these free symbols reduces to the identity, and trace zero otherwise. This trace rule applies inside the given regular representation. Indeed, for $H=\langle p_1,\ldots,p_m\rangle$, the spaces $\ell^2(Ht)$, as $Ht$ ranges over the right cosets of $H$, are reducing copies of the left regular representation of $H$. The vector $\delta_e$ lies in the copy $\ell^2(H)$. Its vector state therefore agrees with the canonical trace of $H$ on polynomials and on the spectral projections under consideration.

<a id="eq:cocycle-moment-expansion"></a>
Fix an integer $r\geq 1$. Expanding $Q_m^r$ gives $$\label{eq:cocycle-moment-expansion}
  \tau_\Gamma(Q_m^r)
  =\sum_{i_1,\ldots,i_{2r}\in\{0,\ldots,m\}}
    \tau_\Gamma\bigl(
      V_{i_1}V_{i_2}^*\cdots V_{i_{2r-1}}V_{i_{2r}}^*
    \bigr).$$ Each summand is zero or one. In a summand equal to one, every nonzero index used must occur at least twice, since a generator occurring only once cannot disappear under free reduction. There are therefore at most $r$ distinct nonzero indices.

If exactly $r$ distinct nonzero indices occur, all $2r$ positions are nonidentity and each index occurs exactly twice. The two occurrences have opposite exponents, as follows already by abelianizing the identity word. Pair the two positions with the same index. This matching is unique for the chosen word. A sequence of adjacent inverse cancellations produces a noncrossing matching: a pair deleted after other pairs can only enclose pairs already deleted, and two deleted pairs cannot cross. Conversely, a noncrossing matching with opposite exponents at its endpoints reduces to the identity by deleting an innermost pair and proceeding by induction. Every noncrossing perfect matching of $\{1,\ldots,2r\}$ has opposite parity at the two endpoints of each pair. To see this, the positions strictly inside that pair must be matched among themselves, so their number is even. The signs in <a href="#eq:cocycle-moment-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-moment-expansion">[eq:cocycle-moment-expansion]</a> alternate; therefore every noncrossing perfect matching has the required opposite signs.

The number of noncrossing perfect matchings is $$\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r=\frac{1}{r+1}\binom{2r}{r}.$$ For completeness, splitting a matching at the partner of its first position gives the recurrence $c_0=1$ and $c_r=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}c_jc_{r-1-j}$. Its formal generating series satisfies $C(z)=1+zC(z)^2$, so the solution with constant term one is $(1-\sqrt{1-4z})/(2z)$; the binomial expansion gives the displayed coefficient $\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r$. For each matching, labeling its $r$ pairs with distinct indices from $\{1,\ldots,m\}$ gives $$(m)_r=m(m-1)\cdots(m-r+1)$$ choices, with the value zero when $m<r$. Ordering the pairs by their first endpoints makes this count unambiguous, and the uniqueness of the equal-index matching prevents double counting. Thus the contribution from exactly $r$ distinct indices is $\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r(m)_r$.

<a id="eq:cocycle-catalan-moments"></a>
Every remaining contributing term has at most $r-1$ distinct nonzero indices. This includes every term with an identity summand: such a term has at most $2r-1$ nonidentity positions, while each index it uses occurs at least twice. For fixed $r$, there are only finitely many patterns specifying the identity positions and the partition of the other positions into equal-index classes. A pattern with $k\leq r-1$ classes has at most $m^k$ labelings. Summing over those patterns gives an $O_r(m^{r-1})$ bound, with its constant depending only on $r$. We have proved $$\label{eq:cocycle-catalan-moments}
  \tau_\Gamma(Q_m^r)
    =\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r(m)_r+O_r(m^{r-1}),
  \qquad
  \int x^r\,d\mu_m(x)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r.$$

<a id="eq:cocycle-subsequence-moments"></a>
We next justify passage from moments to weak convergence. For $r=1$, direct expansion gives $\tau_\Gamma(Q_m)=m+1$, so the first moments of $\mu_m$ are bounded by two. Markov’s inequality gives tightness on $[0,\infty)$. For each fixed integer $r\geq 1$, the moment convergence in <a href="#eq:cocycle-catalan-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-catalan-moments">[eq:cocycle-catalan-moments]</a>, applied also at $r+1$, gives $$\sup_m\int x^{r+1}\,d\mu_m(x)<\infty,
  \qquad
  \sup_m\int_{(R,\infty)}x^r\,d\mu_m(x)
    \leq \frac{1}{R}\sup_m\int x^{r+1}\,d\mu_m(x)
    \longrightarrow 0$$ as $R\to\infty$. Consequently, if a subsequence converges weakly to a probability measure $\mu$, its moments pass to the limit. More explicitly, apply weak convergence to the bounded continuous functions $\min\{x^r,R^r\}$ and use the displayed tail bound; the corresponding bound for $\mu$ follows by first applying weak convergence to bounded truncations of $x^{r+1}$. Letting $R\to\infty$ proves $$\label{eq:cocycle-subsequence-moments}
  \int x^r\,d\mu(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r\qquad(r\geq 0).$$

The density in <a href="#eq:cocycle-limit-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-limit-density">[eq:cocycle-limit-density]</a> realizes these moments, including the zeroth moment. Write $B(a,b)=\int_0^1t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}\,dt$ for the Euler beta integral. The substitution $x=4t$ and the beta integral give, for every integer $r\geq 0$, $$\begin{aligned}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^4 x^{r-1/2}(4-x)^{1/2}\,dx
    &=\frac{4^{r+1}}{2\pi}
      B\left(r+\frac12,\frac32\right)\\
    &=\frac{(2r)!}{r!(r+1)!}
      =\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r.
\end{aligned}$$ The middle equality follows from $B(\tfrac12,\tfrac32)=\pi/2$ and the recurrence $B(a+1,b)=aB(a,b)/(a+b)$. In particular the density has total mass one.

These moments determine a probability measure on $[0,\infty)$ uniquely. Indeed, $\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r\leq 4^r$. If a measure satisfying <a href="#eq:cocycle-subsequence-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-subsequence-moments">[eq:cocycle-subsequence-moments]</a> had mass $c>0$ on $[4+\epsilon,\infty)$ for some $\epsilon>0$, then $$4^r\geq\mathop{\mathrm{Cat}}_r\geq c(4+\epsilon)^r$$ for every $r$, a contradiction for large $r$. Its support is therefore contained in $[0,4]$. On this compact interval, uniform polynomial approximation of continuous functions shows that equality of all moments implies equality of the measures. Every subsequential weak limit is consequently $\nu$. Tightness now gives weak convergence of the full sequence $\mu_m$ to $\nu$. Finally, $\nu$ has the displayed integrable density and thus has no atom at zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Cutoff inversion and finite coefficients

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cocycle-spectrum-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cocycle-spectrum</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:small-cocycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-cocycle">12</a>.* Use the operators $T_m,Q_m$ and the measures $\mu_m$ above. Since $\nu(\{0\})=0$, choose a fixed $d>0$ so small that $$\nu([0,d])<\frac{\eta^2}{16}.$$ The interval $[0,d]$ is closed, so weak convergence and the Portmanteau inequality give $$\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mu_m([0,d])
     \leq\nu([0,d])<\frac{\eta^2}{16}.$$ We may therefore choose $m$ sufficiently large that both <a id="eq:cocycle-cutoff-choices"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-cutoff-choices}
  \mu_m([0,d])<\frac{\eta^2}{8},
  \qquad
  \frac{1}{dm}<\frac{\eta^2}{16}$$ hold. Keep this $d$ and this $m$ fixed for the remainder of the construction.

<a id="eq:cocycle-cutoff-error"></a>
<a id="eq:cocycle-cutoff-size"></a>
Set $$E_m=1_{(dm,\infty)}(Q_m),
  \qquad R_m=f_m(Q_m),
  \qquad
  f_m(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    0,&0\leq x\leq dm,\\
    x^{-1},&x>dm.
  \end{cases}$$ Thus $R_m$ is a bounded positive operator, with $\left\lVert R_m\right\rVert\leq (dm)^{-1}$. The spectral theorem gives <a id="eq:cocycle-cutoff-identities"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-cutoff-identities}
  Q_m R_m=E_m,
  \qquad R_m Q_m R_m=R_m,
  \qquad
  \tau_\Gamma(1-E_m)=\mu_m([0,d]).$$ In particular, these operations allow a kernel or arbitrarily small nonzero spectrum of $Q_m$. Define the coefficient vector $$h^{(0)}=T_m^*R_m\delta_e\in\ell^2(\Gamma).$$ Using $Q_m=T_mT_m^*$ in the indicated order gives <a id="eq:cocycle-cutoff-target"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:cocycle-cutoff-target}
  T_mh^{(0)}&=E_m\delta_e,\\
\label{eq:cocycle-cutoff-error}
  \|T_mh^{(0)}-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}^2
    &=\tau_\Gamma(1-E_m)<\frac{\eta^2}{8},\\
\label{eq:cocycle-cutoff-size}
  \|h^{(0)}\|_{\ell^2}^2
    &=\langle R_mQ_mR_m\delta_e,\delta_e\rangle
      =\tau_\Gamma(R_m)
      \leq\frac{1}{dm}<\frac{\eta^2}{16}.
\end{aligned}$$

The vector $h^{(0)}$ has real coefficients. To check this directly, let $J$ denote coefficientwise complex conjugation on $\ell^2(\Gamma)$. Each left shift is real in the standard basis, so $J$ commutes with $T_m,T_m^*$ and $Q_m$, and $J\delta_e=\delta_e$. Commutation with real polynomials in $Q_m$ extends by uniform approximation to real continuous functions of $Q_m$. The continuous functions $$x\longmapsto \min\{1,\max\{0,l(x-dm)\}\},
  \qquad l=1,2,\ldots,$$ converge pointwise, with a uniform bound, to $1_{(dm,\infty)}(x)$ on the spectrum of $Q_m$. Their functional calculi converge strongly to $E_m$, hence $J$ also commutes with $E_m$. Finally, $$R_m=E_m g_m(Q_m),
  \qquad g_m(x)=\frac{1}{\max\{dm,x\}},$$ where $g_m$ is real and continuous on $[0,\infty)$. Thus $J$ commutes with $R_m$ as well, proving $Jh^{(0)}=h^{(0)}$.

Real finitely supported coefficient vectors are dense in the real subspace of $\ell^2(\Gamma)$. Choose such a vector $h$ with $$\|h-h^{(0)}\|_{\ell^2}
       <\frac{\eta}{4(m+1)}.$$ The elementary bound $\left\lVert T_m\right\rVert\leq m+1$ and <a href="#eq:cocycle-cutoff-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-cutoff-error">[eq:cocycle-cutoff-error]</a>–<a href="#eq:cocycle-cutoff-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-cutoff-size">[eq:cocycle-cutoff-size]</a> yield $$\begin{aligned}
  \|h\|_{\ell^2}
    &<\frac{\eta}{4}+\frac{\eta}{4(m+1)}<\eta,\\
  \|T_mh-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}
    &<\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{8}}+\frac{\eta}{4}<\eta.
\end{aligned}$$ For this finitely supported $h$, form the cocycle with $h_1=h$ and all other generator values zero, and take $w=w_m$. Equation <a href="#eq:cocycle-word-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cocycle-word-value">[eq:cocycle-word-value]</a> gives $D_w=T_mh$, proving <a href="#eq:small-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-cocycle">[eq:small-cocycle]</a>.

The choices have been made in the order $d$, then $m$, then the finite coefficient approximation. The last approximation uses the norm of the already fixed operator $T_m$; the lemma requires no bound on the support size of $h$ uniform in $\eta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:generation"></a>
### Perturbation and a generating limit

We combine the flow estimate with the small-cocycle construction to approximate the extra generator by a word in a slightly perturbed $n$-tuple. We then arrange these approximations so that the limiting $n$-tuple generates the entire von Neumann algebra. We call $C$ a *completion* of $A=(A_1,\ldots,A_n)$ when $(A,C)$ is a freely generating Haar tuple in $M$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### One-step perturbation

<a id="stmt-prop-perturbation"></a>
<div id="prop:perturbation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:perturbation"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Approximating the extra generator in one step). *Let $n\geq 3$, and let $(A_1,\ldots,A_n,C)$ be a freely generating Haar tuple in a finite von Neumann algebra $(M,\tau)$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there are a trace-preserving normal automorphism $\alpha$ of $M$ and a word $w\in\mathbb F_n$ such that, with $A_j'=\alpha(A_j)$, $$\label{eq:perturbation}
 \max_{1\leq j\leq n}\left\lVert A_j'-A_j\right\rVert<\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert A_w'-C\right\rVert<\varepsilon.$$ The tuple $(A_1',\ldots,A_n',C')$, where $C'=\alpha(C)$, is again freely generating Haar. Here the target in the second estimate is the original $C$; the new completion is $$C'=C A_w^*,$$ where $A_w$ is evaluated on the original $n$-tuple.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-perturbation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-perturbation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $0<\eta<\varepsilon/K$, with $K=3\pi$ as in <a href="#eq:free-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-sum">[eq:free-sum]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:small-cocycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-cocycle">12</a> gives a finitely supported real coefficient vector $h_1$, with $h_j=0$ for $j\geq2$, and a word $w\in\mathbb F_n$ for which $$\|h_1\|_{\ell^2}<\eta,
 \qquad
 \|D_w-\delta_e\|_{\ell^2}<\eta.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow">8</a> to these coefficients and write $\beta=\beta_1$. Its displacement estimates <a href="#eq:generator-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:generator-displacement">[eq:generator-displacement]</a> and <a href="#eq:word-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-displacement">[eq:word-displacement]</a> give $$\max_j\left\lVert \beta(A_j)-A_j\right\rVert<\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert \beta(A_w)-C A_w\right\rVert<\varepsilon.$$ Moreover, $\beta$ fixes $S$ and therefore fixes $C=\exp(\mathrm iS)$.

The free-group basis transformation that fixes the first $n$ symbols and sends the last symbol $c$ to $c w$ is invertible: its inverse fixes the first $n$ symbols and sends $c$ to $c w^{-1}$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:haar-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:haar-identification">4</a>, it induces a trace-preserving normal automorphism $\gamma$ of $M$ satisfying $$\gamma(A_j)=A_j \quad(1\leq j\leq n),
 \qquad
 \gamma(C)=C A_w.$$ Set $\alpha=\gamma^{-1}\circ\beta$. Since $\gamma$ fixes every $A_j$ and hence $A_w$, we have the exact identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \alpha(A_j)-A_j
   &=\gamma^{-1}\bigl(\beta(A_j)-A_j\bigr),\\
 \alpha(A_w)-C
   &=\gamma^{-1}\bigl(\beta(A_w)-C A_w\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ An automorphism is isometric, so these identities prove <a href="#eq:perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation">[eq:perturbation]</a>, with the displayed order of the factors. The image under $\alpha$ of the original freely generating Haar tuple is again such a tuple. Finally, $$C'=\alpha(C)=\gamma^{-1}(C)=C A_w^*,$$ because $\beta(C)=C$ and $\gamma(C A_w^*)=C A_w A_w^*=C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### A generating limit

<a id="eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz"></a>
We record the elementary continuity estimate that will control the iteration. For two $d$-tuples of unitaries $U=(U_1,\ldots,U_d)$ and $V=(V_1,\ldots,V_d)$, put $$d_2(U,V)=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\left\lVert U_j-V_j\right\rVert_2.$$ If $v$ is a group word of length $|v|$, telescoping its product gives $$\left\lVert U_v-V_v\right\rVert_2\leq |v|\,d_2(U,V).$$ Indeed, multiplication on either side by a unitary preserves the tracial $L^2$ norm, and $\left\lVert U_j^*-V_j^*\right\rVert_2=\left\lVert U_j-V_j\right\rVert_2$. Consequently, a finite word polynomial $F=\sum_{\nu=1}^q c_\nu v_\nu$ satisfies $$\label{eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz}
 \left\lVert F(U)-F(V)\right\rVert_2
 \leq L(F)d_2(U,V),
 \qquad
 L(F)=\sum_{\nu=1}^q |c_\nu|\,|v_\nu|.$$ The same telescoping argument also gives continuity in operator norm.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-perturbation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-perturbation</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:rank-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rank-step">1</a>.* Fix $n\geq3$ and let $M=L(\mathbb F_{n+1})$, with its canonical normalized trace and canonical freely generating Haar tuple $$(A_1^{(0)},\ldots,A_n^{(0)},C^{(0)}).$$ Write $A^{(k)}=(A_1^{(k)},\ldots,A_n^{(k)})$. Choose a sequence $(y_j)_{j\geq1}$ that is dense in $L^2(M,\tau)$ and consists of elements of the rational complex group word algebra of the initial tuple. Such a sequence exists by Lemma <a href="#lem:haar-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:haar-identification">4</a>; coefficients in $\mathbb{Q}+\mathrm i\mathbb{Q}$ make this algebra countable. Put $r_0=1$.

We construct freely generating Haar tuples $(A^{(k)},C^{(k)})$ and positive numbers $r_k$ by induction. Suppose the construction has reached stage $k-1$, where $k\geq1$. The choices at stage $k$ are made in the following order.

<a id="eq:stage-old-approximation"></a>
<a id="eq:stage-tolerance"></a>
First, for each $1\leq j\leq k$, choose a finite linear combination $F_{j,k}$ of group words in $n+1$ variables such that $$\label{eq:stage-old-approximation}
 \left\lVert 
 F_{j,k}(A^{(k-1)},C^{(k-1)})-y_j
 \right\rVert_2<2^{-k}.$$ This is possible because the current completed tuple generates $M$ and its group word algebra is dense in $L^2(M,\tau)$. Let $H_k\geq0$ be a common bound for the finitely many constants $L(F_{j,k})$ in <a href="#eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz">[eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz]</a>. Choose $$\label{eq:stage-tolerance}
 0<\varepsilon_k<
 \min\left\{\frac{r_{k-1}}2,\,
                  \frac{2^{-k}}{1+H_k}\right\}.$$

<a id="eq:stage-perturbation"></a>
Next apply Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">15</a> to $(A^{(k-1)},C^{(k-1)})$ with tolerance $\varepsilon_k$. Let $\alpha_k$ and $w_k\in\mathbb F_n$ be its automorphism and word, and set $$A_j^{(k)}=\alpha_k(A_j^{(k-1)}),
 \qquad
 C^{(k)}=\alpha_k(C^{(k-1)}).$$ The new completed tuple is freely generating Haar, and $$\label{eq:stage-perturbation}
 \max_j\left\lVert A_j^{(k)}-A_j^{(k-1)}\right\rVert<\varepsilon_k,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert A_{w_k}^{(k)}-C^{(k-1)}\right\rVert<\varepsilon_k.$$ In particular, the last expression is close to the old completion, as needed in <a href="#eq:stage-old-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-old-approximation">[eq:stage-old-approximation]</a>.

<a id="eq:stage-new-approximation"></a>
Now define word polynomials in $n$ variables by $$G_{j,k}(X)=F_{j,k}(X,X_{w_k})
 \qquad(1\leq j\leq k).$$ Since $\left\lVert x\right\rVert_2\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert$, equations <a href="#eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz">[eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz]</a>, <a href="#eq:stage-old-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-old-approximation">[eq:stage-old-approximation]</a>, and <a href="#eq:stage-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-perturbation">[eq:stage-perturbation]</a> imply $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:stage-new-approximation}
 \left\lVert G_{j,k}(A^{(k)})-y_j\right\rVert_2
 &\leq
 \left\lVert F_{j,k}(A^{(k)},A_{w_k}^{(k)})
       -F_{j,k}(A^{(k-1)},C^{(k-1)})\right\rVert_2
 \notag\\
 &\qquad
 +\left\lVert F_{j,k}(A^{(k-1)},C^{(k-1)})-y_j\right\rVert_2
 \notag\\
 &<H_k\varepsilon_k+2^{-k}<2\cdot2^{-k}.
\end{aligned}$$ This estimate uses $A_{w_k}^{(k)}$ as a single unitary slot of $F_{j,k}$. Thus the tolerance chosen in <a href="#eq:stage-tolerance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-tolerance">[eq:stage-tolerance]</a> does not depend on the length of the word that the perturbation subsequently produces.

<a id="eq:future-budget"></a>
Finally, let $L_k\geq0$ be a common $L^2$-Lipschitz bound for the finitely many polynomials $G_{j,k}$, and choose $$\label{eq:future-budget}
 0<r_k\leq
 \min\left\{\frac{r_{k-1}}2,\,
                  \frac{2^{-k}}{1+L_k}\right\}.$$ These constants are finite by <a href="#eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz">[eq:word-polynomial-lipschitz]</a>. They are chosen after $w_k$ is known, so any increase in word length is accommodated in the budget for future steps. Both quantities in the minimum are positive, and the induction can continue.

Figure <a href="#fig:iteration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:iteration">1</a> records this order of choices and the role of the budget in controlling all later perturbations.

<figure id="fig:iteration">

<figcaption>The choices at stage $k$ follow the arrows. The witness word $w_{k}$ is found only after $ε_{k}$ is fixed. A longer witness word may increase the Lipschitz bound $L_{k}$ of the substituted polynomials $G_{j, k}$, so its length affects the future budget $r_{k}$ only. This budget bounds the total change after stage $k$ and preserves the target approximations in the limit.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-iteration"></a>

]
\node[stage] (old) {$F_{j,k}$\\[3pt]
  Approximate $y_1,\ldots,y_k$\\in the current tuple};
\node[stage, right=of old] (epsilon) {$\varepsilon_k$\\[3pt]
  Choose the tolerance\\from $H_k$ and $r_{k-1}$};
\node[stage, right=of epsilon] (word) {$\alpha_k,\ w_k$\\[3pt]
  Perturb the tuple;\\approximate the old $C^{(k-1)}$};
\node[stage, below=of word] (new) {$G_{j,k}$\\[3pt]
  Substitute the witness word:\\
  $F_{j,k}(X,X_{w_k})$};
\node[stage, below=of epsilon] (budget) {$L_k,\ r_k$\\[3pt]
  Choose a\\Lipschitz bound;\\fix the future budget};
\node[stage, below=of old] (tail) {Tail control\\[3pt]
  $\sum_{\ell>k}\varepsilon_\ell\leq r_k$\\
  Preserve earlier approximations};
\draw[choice] (old) -- (epsilon);
\draw[choice] (epsilon) -- (word);
\draw[choice] (word) -- (new);

\end{tikzpicture}
